## Genesis 25:21 (id: 12512)

* For 20 years, Rebekah could not have children (compare [25:20](https://ref.ly/Gen25:20), [26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:26)), similar to Sarah (see [16:1](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1)). This condition tested their faith (see study note on 16:1–3). How could they be childless when God promised that nations would come from them?
* Isaac begged the Lord for help, and the Lord answered his prayer. Isaac seemed to learn from his father's mistake and acted with faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:21
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 16:1-3 (ID: `12348@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 25:22 (id: 12513)

When Rebekah’s pregnancy was difficult, she went to ask the LORD about it. She likely did this by visiting a prophet (a person who speaks God’s message), possibly Abraham ([20:7](https://ref.ly/Gen20:7); see study note on 25:7–8).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:22
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:7-8 (ID: `12527@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 25:23 (id: 12514)

* *nations:* Jacob and Esau fought in the womb, and their descendants (Israel and Edom) fought continuously throughout their history. In their many conflicts, Israel achieved supremacy over Edom.
* *the older will serve younger:* God chose the younger son over the elder, which went against the usual order (compare [48:12–14](https://ref.ly/Gen48:12-Gen48:14); see [Malachi 1:1–3](https://ref.ly/Mal1:1-Mal1:3); [Romans 9:11–16](https://ref.ly/Rom9:11-Rom9:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 25:24–26 (id: 12515)

When the twins were born, the unusual circumstances inspired each boy’s name and hinted at what would happen to them in the future.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:24-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`)

## Genesis 25:25 (id: 12516)

* Two wordplays anticipate Esau's future. *Esau* sounds like the Hebrew word *se‘ar*, meaning "hair." Esau's later homeland was Edom. Another name for it was *Seir* ("hairy") because it was wooded (as though covered with hair).
* *red* (Hebrew *’admoni*): This Hebrew word sounds like Esau’s other name, Edom, which had *red* soil ([25:27–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:27-Gen25:34)).
* *hair like a fur coat:* This suggests his unspiritual character ([25:34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:34); [Hebrews 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16); compare [Leviticus 26:22](https://ref.ly/Lev26:22); [Deuteronomy 7:22](https://ref.ly/Deut7:22); [1 Corinthians 15:32](https://ref.ly/1Cor15:32)). The description of the child uses words that emphasize the Edomites' character.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`)

## Genesis 25:26 (id: 12517)

The name Jacob (Hebrew *ya‘aqob*, from Hebrew *‘aqeb*, “heel, footprint”) was originally positive, meaning “protect” (like a rear guard), but it took on the negative meaning of “heel grabber” or “deceiver” in the context of Jacob’s deceptive, grasping, usurping character (see [27:36](https://ref.ly/Gen27:36)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 25:27 (id: 12519)

* *Esau became a skilled hunter, a man of the field:* He was a wild man who enjoyed the wild country.
* *Jacob was a quiet man who stayed at home* (literally *a mature/perfect man dwelling in tents*): He lived in tents and had good manners.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`)

## Genesis 25:27–34 (id: 12518)

Jacob and Esau developed according to their early traits ([25:24–26](https://ref.ly/Gen25:24-Gen25:26)). 

Esau, the reddish, hairy man, cared about physical things rather than spiritual things (see [Hebrews 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)). His desires eventually overcame him and he sold his birthright. 

Jacob, the heel grabber, knew the birthright’s value. He made a ruthless deal to win it from his brother.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:27-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Lentil (ID: `flora:Lentil`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`)

## Genesis 25:28 (id: 12520)

* Each parent had a favorite child.
* *Because:* Isaac loved Esau because of his hunting ability. Rebekah had a constant and unconditional love for Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Genesis 25:29 (id: 12521)

* Jacob was actually more cunning than Esau. The word for *cooking* (Hebrew *wayyazed*, meaning “boiling”) sounds like the word for *hunter* (Hebrew *tsayid*). While Jacob was boiling stew, he was also setting a trap for Esau, who is described as hairy and red. He may have waited a long time for this moment.

    The word for *cooking* can also describe acting without proper limits, like water boiling over a pot. In this way, Jacob went too far when he took the promise for himself. By contrast, Abraham trusted that the promise was already his, so he was willing to give the land to others (see study note on 13:8–13).

* Esau was tired and hungry, but his life was not in danger ([25:32](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32); see study note on 25:33–34).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:29
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 13:8-13 (ID: `12316@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Genesis 25:33-34 (ID: `12524@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 25:30 (id: 12522)

* Esau focused only on his hunger. When a person is controlled by physical desires like this, there is no room for spiritual values. The text shows this clearly by using a Hebrew word (translated *"Let me eat"*) that was often used when feeding animals.
* *Edom* means “red.” See study note on 25:25.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:30
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:25 (ID: `12516@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 25:31–33 (id: 12523)

On this occasion, Jacob proved to be the better hunter. However, strong ambition like this can be dangerous. God’s people should desire what God desires, but they must not try to gain these things by human effort (see [Zechariah 4:6](https://ref.ly/Zech4:6); [Galatians 5:16–17](https://ref.ly/Gal5:16-Gal5:17); [Ephesians 6:10–12](https://ref.ly/Eph6:10-Eph6:12)). 

The Lord dealt strictly with Jacob to remove his sinful methods. Later, he received the promise not as Jacob the deceiver, but as Israel (which means “God fights”). God fought on his behalf ([Genesis 32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:31-33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`)

## Genesis 25:33–34 (id: 12524)

Esau quickly accepted what Jacob offered and fell into his trap. He ate and left so quickly that he could not have been close to death ([25:32](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32)). The final comment in the passage explains that Esau treated his birthright with contempt, as if it had no value ([Hebrews 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)). It is foolish to give up spiritual blessings just to satisfy physical desires (compare [Genesis 3:6](https://ref.ly/Gen3:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 25:33-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Lentil (ID: `flora:Lentil`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Stew (ID: `realia:Stew`)

## Genesis 26:1 (id: 12533)

This Abimelech is probably not the same person from chapter [20](https://ref.ly/Gen20:1-Gen20:18) because these events could be 90 years apart. Abimelech might have been the name of a dynasty or a title. [First Samuel 21:10](https://ref.ly/1Sam21:10) mentions a later King Achish, who was also called Abimelech([Psalm 34:title](https://ref.ly/Ps34:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 26:1–35 (id: 12528)

This section pauses Jacob’s story to focus on Isaac. Isaac’s success and wealth in chapter [26](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:35) show that God’s blessing had passed to him (compare [25:11](https://ref.ly/Gen25:11)), even though he sometimes failed to trust God.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:1-35

## Genesis 26:2–5 (id: 12534)

The Lord assured Isaac that the covenant promises (compare [12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3); [15:5–8](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5-Gen15:8); [17:3–8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:3-Gen17:8); [22:15–18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:15-Gen22:18); [28:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen28:13-Gen28:14)) would pass to him because Abraham faithfully listened to God and obeyed all his requirements, commands, decrees, and instructions. These terms were later used in Deuteronomy to describe God’s full legal covenant with Israel.

An Israelite would immediately think of the complete Torah when hearing these words and be prompted to obey God’s law as Abraham did, though Abraham had only a few commands from the Lord. Through these words, the text emphasizes that Abraham would have obeyed the later commands if he had had them, because he was an obedient servant of the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:2-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Genesis 26:6–11 (id: 12539)

While Isaac was living in Gerar, he, like his father Abraham, told people that his wife was his sister. Some think this story repeats the accounts of Abraham’s deception ([12:10–20](https://ref.ly/Gen12:10-Gen12:20); [20:1–18](https://ref.ly/Gen20:1-Gen20:18)). But there are more differences than similarities. It is not surprising that a son repeated his father’s lie.

[Genesis 26](https://ref.ly/Gen26:1-Gen26:35) shows, through many similar patterns, that God’s plan continued through Isaac. Even when Isaac put the covenant at risk, as Abraham had done, God prevented something terrible from happening and protected the marriage. God would bless Abraham’s descendants because of Abraham, but they still needed to trust God themselves to experience those blessings. 

True faith in God's promises leads to a life without fear as one follows him. Fear, however, can put the blessing at risk and make a person’s faith seem empty.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:6-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Genesis 26:8 (id: 12540)

*Abimelech … looked down …* *to see Isaac caressing his wife Rebekah:* The Hebrew word for "caressing" is the same as the word used for Ishmael "mocking" Isaac ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Gen21:9)). This Hebrew word is also related to the name Isaac (Hebrew *yitskhaq*). It is as if Isaac’s lack of faith made Abimelech look foolish and brought shame on Rebekah and on the great promise connected to Isaac’s name.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Genesis 26:10–11 (id: 12529)

Isaac, like his father, was rebuked by Abimelech (see study note on 20:1–18). This legal wording would remind Israel of how important it was to preserve marital purity. Abimelech recognized the danger to his own people. Though his decree preserved his own society, it was also a word from God that preserved the sanctity of Isaac’s and Israel’s marriages. If Isaac’s marriage had ended here, there would have been no Israelite society.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:10-11
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 20:1-18 (ID: `12419@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`)

## Genesis 26:12–13 (id: 12530)

Isaac lived in the land as a temporary resident. God blessed him, so his crops grew well and he became very rich.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:12-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 26:14–16 (id: 12531)

The Philistines were jealous of Isaac's success and filled his wells with dirt. The king then told Isaac to leave the area because he was too powerful for them (compare [21:22–23](https://ref.ly/Gen21:22-Gen21:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:14-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Abimelech (ID: `person:Abimelech`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Genesis 26:17–22 (id: 12532)

*Isaac left … in the Valley of Gerar:* This was away from the city of Gerar itself, but probably still within 16 kilometers (10 miles). 

When he got there, *Isaac reopened the wells of his father*. Although people opposed him there, Isaac chose not to fight back. He gave up one well after another until God's blessing overcame the opposition. Whenever Isaac reopened a well, even if enemies filled it in, he found water. God blessed Isaac, and nothing could stop it. Finally, the Philistines left Isaac alone.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:17-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Gerar (ID: `place:Gerar`); Esek (ID: `place:Esek`); Sitnah (ID: `place:Sitnah`); Rehoboth (ID: `place:Rehoboth`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 26:26–33 (id: 12536)

This oath is like the one a previous king made with Abraham (see [21:22–31](https://ref.ly/Gen21:22-Gen21:31)). This king acknowledged that God was blessing Isaac. He understood that an oath with Isaac would be beneficial. 

No opposition can stop God's blessing. It will flourish, and other nations will notice it. They will seek peace with God's people to share in the blessing.

## Genesis 26:34–35 (id: 12538)

Esau's marriages show how he was not suitable to lead God's covenant people into receiving God's blessings. Isaac's later attempt to bless Esau was foolish ([27:1–40](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:40)). Later, Esau married a third wife, trying to make things right without success ([28:6–9](https://ref.ly/Gen28:6-Gen28:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 26:34-35

## Genesis 27:1–4 (id: 12542)

* The first scene introduces the main problem in the chapter. Isaac knew about God’s oracle (a message from God; [25:22–23](https://ref.ly/Gen25:22-Gen25:23)), but he went against it or ignored it when he tried to bless Esau.
* *Isaac was old and his eyes were so weak that he could no longer see:* This shows that he was losing awareness, both physically and spiritually.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:1-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Quiver (ID: `realia:Quiver`); Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`)

## Genesis 27:1–40 (id: 12541)

Jacob got his father Isaac’s blessing through deception. In this story, an entire family tries to carry out their responsibilities by physical means rather than by faith. Faith would have provided Rebekah and Jacob a more honorable solution to the crisis.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:1-40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Kid (ID: `fauna:Kid`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`); Must (ID: `realia:Must`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); To Curse (ID: `keyterm:Curse`); Quiver (ID: `realia:Quiver`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Yoke (ID: `realia:Yoke`); Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 27:3–4 (id: 12547)

Like Esau, Isaac let his love of tasty food control his affections (compare [25:28–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:28-Gen25:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Quiver (ID: `realia:Quiver`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Bow (ID: `realia:Bow`)

## Genesis 27:5 (id: 12558)

Esau agreed to Isaac’s plan. In doing so, he broke the oath he had sworn to Jacob ([25:33](https://ref.ly/Gen25:33)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 27:5–17 (id: 12557)

The blessing seemed to be at risk. In the second scene, Rebekah and Jacob tried to gain God’s blessing through deception, rather than through faith and love. Rebekah made a plan to trick Isaac, who was old, into thinking he was blessing Esau when he was actually blessing Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:5-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Kid (ID: `fauna:Kid`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 27:11–12 (id: 12543)

Jacob had no problem tricking his father. He only worried it might fail and Isaac would curse him for attempting it.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:11-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

Jacob lied about his identity, and then came close to blasphemy (speaking in a way that dishonors God) by lying about God.

In scene three, Jacob tricks his father and receives the blessing.

## Genesis 27:33 (id: 12549)

When Isaac understood what happened, he started shaking uncontrollably. He had tried to change God's plan, but God stopped him.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 27:34–35 (id: 12550)

Esau was very bitter and angry enough to kill. After Jacob received their father’s blessing, Esau planned to take his brother’s life ([27:41](https://ref.ly/Gen27:41)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:34-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`)

## Genesis 27:36 (id: 12551)

* *he has cheated me twice* (or *tripped me up,* or *deceived me*): Esau started to understand Jacob's true nature. Jacob took the *birthright* (the right of inheritance, Hebrew *bekorah*; [25:27–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:27-Gen25:34)). He tricked their father to get the *blessing* (the spoken pronouncement of the inheritance, Hebrew *berakah*).
* Esau was right in how he judged Jacob, but he did not recognize his own ungodliness in these actions (see study note on 27:5; [Hebrews 12:16](https://ref.ly/Heb12:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:36
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 27:5 (ID: `12558@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 27:37 (id: 12552)

There was no way to undo the blessing. Isaac had declared a message from God, who had made Jacob to be Esau’s master (see [Romans 9:11–13](https://ref.ly/Rom9:11-Rom9:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`); Must (ID: `realia:Must`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Genesis 27:39–40 (id: 12553)

All that remained for Esau was a promise of hardship and struggle (compare [3:17–19](https://ref.ly/Gen3:17-Gen3:19); [16:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11-Gen16:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:39-40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Yoke (ID: `realia:Yoke`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Genesis 27:41–45 (id: 12554)

Rebekah and Jacob received the blessing, but Esau hated them for it. They also separated from one another. The Bible does not indicate that Rebekah and Jacob ever saw each other again. 

They gained nothing that God would not have given them anyway. Their actions came at the expense of their relationship. Jacob had to flee from home to escape Esau's vengeance.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:41-45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Haran (ID: `place:Haran`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`)

## Genesis 27:42–33.17 (id: 12555)

Jacob met the Lord on a road that Abraham had traveled 125 years earlier. The story in [27:42–33:17](https://ref.ly/Gen27:42-Gen33:17) follows a pattern (called a "chiasm") focusing on Jacob’s exile: 

* A: Jacob runs away from Esau ([27:42–28:9](https://ref.ly/Gen27:42-Gen28:9))

    + B: Angels of God meet Jacob at Bethel ([28:10–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:10-Gen28:22))

    - C: Jacob lives in exile in Paddan\-aram ([29:1–31:55](https://ref.ly/Gen29:1-Gen31:55))
        + B’: Angels of God meet Jacob at Mahanaim ([32:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen32:1-Gen32:2))
* A’: Jacob reconciles with Esau ([32:3–33:17](https://ref.ly/Gen32:3-Gen33:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:42-45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`); Jegar-sahadutha (ID: `place:Jegar-sahadutha`); Teraphim (ID: `deity:Teraphim`); Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Haran (ID: `place:Haran`); Arameans (ID: `group:Aramean`)

## Genesis 27:46 (id: 12556)

Rebekah convinced Isaac to send Jacob away by carefully planning her words and actions. Like Isaac, Jacob married a woman from his relatives who lived in the east.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 27:46

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 28:1–2 (id: 12559)

* Isaac stayed in the land, but Jacob had to leave. God would deal with Jacob through his uncle Laban (see study note on 29:1–31:55).
* Believers must remain spiritually pure by marrying other believers ([2 Corinthians 6:14–18](https://ref.ly/2Cor6:14-2Cor6:18)). The Canaanites had many groups and clans among their people through wars, treaties, and marriages (see [Genesis 34:20–23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:20-Gen34:23)).

    Abraham’s family was to resist such mixing (compare [24:3](https://ref.ly/Gen24:3); chapter [34](https://ref.ly/Gen34:1-Gen34:31)). They were supposed to marry within their clan to maintain the purity of the line and of the faith that identified them as the chosen people. The surest way to lose their distinctiveness was to intermarry with people of other tribal backgrounds and beliefs (see [Ezra 9–10](https://ref.ly/Ezra9:1-Ezra10:44); [Nehemiah 13:23–29](https://ref.ly/Neh13:23-Neh13:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:1-2
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 29:1-31.55 (ID: `12573@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bethuel (ID: `person:Bethuel`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 28:3–5 (id: 12571)

Before Jacob departed, Isaac gave him a pure, legitimate blessing. He did not hold back, because he now knew what God wanted him to do. Isaac clearly passed on the blessing God Almighty (Hebrew *El\-Shaddai*; see [17:1](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1)) had given to Abraham and to him regarding prosperity and the land (compare [15:5](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5), [18–20](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18-Gen15:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:3-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Arameans (ID: `group:Aramean`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebekah (ID: `person:Rebekah`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Bethuel (ID: `person:Bethuel`)

## Genesis 28:6–9 (id: 12572)

Esau, the unchosen son still trying to please his father, married a woman from the unchosen line of Ishmael, which he thought would be more acceptable. He did not understand the uniqueness of the covenant family.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:6-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Mahalath (ID: `person:Mahalath`); Nebaioth (ID: `person:Nebaioth`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Ishmael (ID: `person:Ishmael`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`)

## Genesis 28:10–22 (id: 12560)

Even though Jacob used deceptive methods to get the blessing, God promised to protect and provide for him. The God of Abraham and Isaac was also the God of Jacob. This revelation greatly changed Jacob's perspective and brought faith into clearer focus.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:10-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Vow (ID: `keyterm:Vow`); Haran (ID: `place:Haran`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Beersheba (ID: `place:Beersheba`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`)

## Genesis 28:11 (id: 12561)

Jacob set up camp in a safe spot at the base of a hill. He probably used a large stone more for protection than as a pillow.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:11


## Genesis 28:12–13 (id: 12562)

* *ladder*: This word occurs nowhere else in Scripture. The image may have reminded people at that time of a stairway or ramp on the front of a ziggurat (a large temple tower in ancient Mesopotamia). Such a structure was thought to connect heaven and earth (see study note on 11:4).

    In this passage, God begins direct communication between heaven and earth to guide and protect Jacob, who carries his covenant ([28:13–15](https://ref.ly/Gen28:13-Gen28:15); see [Psalm 91:11–15](https://ref.ly/Ps91:11-Ps91:15)).

* Jesus later said that he himself is the way between heaven and earth ([John 1:51](https://ref.ly/John1:51)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:12-13
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 11:4 (ID: `12280@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 28:12–15 (id: 12563)

The vision showed that God and his angels were with Jacob on his journey. God reminded Jacob of the promises made to Abraham and Isaac. He promised Jacob land, descendants as numerous as dust (compare [13:16](https://ref.ly/Gen13:16); [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)), and blessings for all people through him (compare [12:2–3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:2-Gen12:3); [15:5](https://ref.ly/Gen15:5), [18](https://ref.ly/Gen15:18); [17:3–8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:3-Gen17:8); [22:15–18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:15-Gen22:18); [35:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen35:11-Gen35:12)). God also promised to be with Jacob and protect him until he returned.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:12-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`)

## Genesis 28:14 (id: 12564)

Jacob inherited Abraham’s entire covenant (see [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)), which confirmed Isaac’s blessing ([28:3–4](https://ref.ly/Gen28:3-Gen28:4)) and showed that Jacob would live away from home for a time ([28:15](https://ref.ly/Gen28:15); see [15:12–16](https://ref.ly/Gen15:12-Gen15:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`)

## Genesis 28:15 (id: 12565)

*I am with you:* The promise of God’s presence meant that God would protect and provide for Jacob in a special way. God’s promise to be with his people is repeated throughout Scripture (see also [26:24](https://ref.ly/Gen26:24)). It encourages those who have faith to respond with worship and confidence ([28:16–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:16-Gen28:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:15


## Genesis 28:16–22 (id: 12566)

The second part of the passage describes Jacob's response to God's revelation. He felt reverence and awe in God's presence. His acts of devotion became models for Israel's worship. 

When God graciously visits his people and promises to be with them and make them a blessing to the world, his people respond in faith with reverence, worship, offerings, and vows. They preserve their faith in memory for future worshipers.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:16-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`); Vow (ID: `keyterm:Vow`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`)

## Genesis 28:18 (id: 12567)

Anointing with oil became a way of setting something apart for divine use in Israel’s worship ([Exodus 29:1–7](https://ref.ly/Exod29:1-Exod29:7); [40:9](https://ref.ly/Exod40:9); [Leviticus 2:1](https://ref.ly/Lev2:1); [1 Samuel 10:1](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`)

## Genesis 28:19 (id: 12568)

*Bethel* later became a holy site for Israel (see [Judges 20:18–27](https://ref.ly/Judg20:18-Judg20:27); [1 Samuel 7:16](https://ref.ly/1Sam7:16); [10:3](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:3); [1 Kings 12:26–13:10](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:26-1Kgs13:10); [2 Kings 2:2–3](https://ref.ly/2Kgs2:2-2Kgs2:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bethel (ID: `place:Bethel`)

## Genesis 28:20–22 (id: 12569)

In view of what God would do for him, Jacob vowed to do certain things for God. He believed the Lord’s words and responded in gratitude. Jacob’s vow influenced Israel’s way of making commitments to God in worship.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:20-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vow (ID: `keyterm:Vow`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 28:22 (id: 12570)

*I will surely You a tenth:* By paying a tithe (*a tenth*) as an act of worship, a person acknowledges that everything is a gift from God and belongs to God (see [14:19–20](https://ref.ly/Gen14:19-Gen14:20); [Numbers 18:21–32](https://ref.ly/Num18:21-Num18:32); [Deuteronomy 14:22–29](https://ref.ly/Deut14:22-Deut14:29); [2 Chronicles 31:5–6](https://ref.ly/2Chr31:5-2Chr31:6); [Malachi 3:7–12](https://ref.ly/Mal3:7-Mal3:12); [Matthew 23:23](https://ref.ly/Matt23:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 28:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 29:1 (id: 12579)

*Jacob resumed his journey:* The Hebrew text can be literally translated as he "lifted up his feet," suggesting he felt inspired. He continued his journey with renewed enthusiasm. This change in attitude came from the vision he received at Bethel. 

It was a wonderful revelation that God would protect and bless him. Now, he expected God's promises to come true, not only to escape from Esau. Jacob’s attitude had become positive and magnanimous to the point of being naive and vulnerable.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 29:1–31.55 (id: 12573)

* These chapters describe how God fulfilled his promise by greatly blessing Jacob with a family and possessions. God also disciplined Jacob, leaving him to struggle with Laban for many years. Laban was Jacob’s match in deception, and thus a means of correction.
* The story of Jacob and Laban is similar to Israel's later stay in Egypt. Jacob struggled while serving his uncle but finally emerged with a large family (the founders of the 12 tribes) and great wealth. In Egypt, the Israelites suffered under their oppressors, but they also flourished, becoming a great nation of 12 large tribes and escaping with great riches.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:1-31:55

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Teraphim (ID: `deity:Teraphim`); Jegar-sahadutha (ID: `place:Jegar-sahadutha`); Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Paddan-aram (ID: `place:Paddan-aram`); Work (ID: `keyterm:Work`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Arameans (ID: `group:Aramean`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Nahor (ID: `person:Nahor.2`); Mandrake (ID: `flora:Mandrake`); Household Idols (ID: `realia:HouseholdIdols`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`); Tent (ID: `realia:Tent`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Gad (ID: `person:Gad`)

## Genesis 29:2–12 (id: 12580)

Jacob met Rachel at the well at just the right time. This happened under God’s guidance, because God rules over all things (God is sovereign). God was leading Jacob toward the fulfillment of his promises (see [24:12–20](https://ref.ly/Gen24:12-Gen24:20)). The well was a reminder of God’s blessing (compare [16:13–14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:13-Gen16:14); [21:19](https://ref.ly/Gen21:19); [26:19–25](https://ref.ly/Gen26:19-Gen26:25), [33](https://ref.ly/Gen26:33)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:2-12

## Genesis 29:10 (id: 12574)

* Unlike the lazy, unhelpful shepherds ([29:7–8](https://ref.ly/Gen29:7-Gen29:8)), this verse shows Jacob to be generous, hardworking, and energetic.
* *watered his uncle's sheep:* Laban's flocks would thrive under Jacob's care.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:10

## Genesis 29:11 (id: 12575)

*Jacob kissed Rachel:* Kissing relatives was an appropriate way to greet them ([29:13](https://ref.ly/Gen29:13); compare [Song 8:1](https://ref.ly/Song8:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`)

## Genesis 29:14 (id: 12577)

*You are indeed my own flesh and blood:* Laban welcomed Jacob into his house and treated him much like a son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`)

## Genesis 29:14–30 (id: 12576)

Jacob looked forward with joy to marrying Rachel, but this hope became a time of testing. Laban acted with shrewdness (clever but not honest behavior), and Jacob was disciplined (corrected and trained). Jacob and his mother had deceived his father and brother to gain the blessing. Now Laban, his mother’s brother, deceived him.

Jacob experienced the results of his own dishonesty through 20 years of hard work, suffering, and being misled while serving Laban ([31:38](https://ref.ly/Gen31:38)). 

In God’s fair judgment, people receive what they do ([Galatians 6:7](https://ref.ly/Gal6:7)). Laban’s actions helped Jacob see his own tendency to deceive others. God often brings people into the lives of believers to discipline them. But Jacob was determined, and God greatly blessed him with a large family and many possessions during this time of service ([30:25–43](https://ref.ly/Gen30:25-Gen30:43)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:14-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Work (ID: `keyterm:Work`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`)

## Genesis 29:18 (id: 12578)

Seven years of service was a high bride\-price in the ancient world, but Rachel was beautiful (like Sarah and Rebekah), and Jacob was in love with her.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 29:23–26 (id: 12581)

* Like Isaac, Jacob was given food and wine (compare [27:25](https://ref.ly/Gen27:25)). He could not see in the darkness (compare [27:1](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1)). He became confused by clothing (compare [27:15](https://ref.ly/Gen27:15)) and misled by touch (compare [27:23](https://ref.ly/Gen27:23)).
* The marriage had already been completed (the husband and wife had come together as one; [29:23](https://ref.ly/Gen29:23); compare [2:24](https://ref.ly/Gen2:24)). Because of this, Jacob was bound to Leah.
* *It is not our custom here to give the younger daughter in marriage before the older**:* Laban's words are a reminder of what Jacob did when he, the younger son, pretended to be his older brother to gain the blessing (chapter [27](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:46)). Now Leah, the older sister, pretended to be the younger sister to get a husband. God gave the deceiver a dose of his own deception as a discipline in his life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:23-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`)

## Genesis 29:28–30 (id: 12582)

After the bridal week ended, Jacob also married Rachel ([29:27](https://ref.ly/Gen29:27)). But he had to work another seven years (see [31:41](https://ref.ly/Gen31:41)). Laban seemed to have the advantage.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:28-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Genesis 29:30 (id: 12583)

*he loved Rachel more than Leah:* This kind of favoritism (showing special love to one person over others) caused ongoing trouble in his family (see [25:28](https://ref.ly/Gen25:28); [37:3](https://ref.ly/Gen37:3)). Jacob’s favoritism had lasting effects. His family was often divided, and their descendants (the tribes of Israel) were rarely united.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`)

## Genesis 29:31–35 (id: 12585)

* Leah’s first four sons were born in rapid succession, but Rachel could not conceive. She was unable to have children, like Sarah and Rebekah (compare [16:1](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1); [25:21](https://ref.ly/Gen25:21); see also [1 Samuel 1:1–28](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:1-1Sam1:28); [Luke 1:5–25](https://ref.ly/Luke1:5-Luke1:25)).
* Each name is a memorable wordplay on Leah’s experience and hopes.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:31-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Genesis 29:31–30.24 (id: 12584)

* The rivalry between these sisters explains much of the later conflict among their sons, and then among the tribes. This is similar to how Jacob and Laban's rivalry predicted future conflicts between Israel and the Arameans of Damascus ([2 Samuel 8:5–6](https://ref.ly/2Sam8:5-2Sam8:6); [10:8–19](https://ref.ly/2Sam10:8-2Sam10:19); [1 Kings 20:1–34](https://ref.ly/1Kgs20:1-1Kgs20:34); [2 Kings 5:1–8:29](https://ref.ly/2Kgs5:1-2Kgs8:29); [13:1–25](https://ref.ly/2Kgs13:1-2Kgs13:25); [Isaiah 7:1–9](https://ref.ly/Isa7:1-Isa7:9)).
* God fights for the cause of the poor and oppressed. He honored Leah, the despised first wife, by making her the first mother. Judah's royal tribe and Levi's priestly line came from her. This was despite Jacob's favoritism for Rachel and her children.

    Despite the tension and jealousy resulting from Laban’s deceit and Jacob’s favoritism, God still grew Jacob’s family and brought about the births of the tribal ancestors.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:31-30:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Mandrake (ID: `flora:Mandrake`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Gad (ID: `person:Gad`); Dan (ID: `person:Dan`); Asher (ID: `person:Asher`); Issachar (ID: `person:Issachar`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Zebulun (ID: `person:Zebulun`); Naphtali (ID: `person:Naphtali`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`)

## Genesis 29:32 (id: 12586)

*The LORD has seen my affliction:* The name *Reuben* (from the Hebrew *re’uben*) sounds like the Hebrew phrase *ra‘ah be‘onyi*, whichmeans “he has seen my affliction.” Leah felt that God had seen her suffering and had comforted her when her son was born, and she hoped that Jacob would now love her. 

Jacob did not notice her suffering, but God did (compare [16:14](https://ref.ly/Gen16:14); [24:62](https://ref.ly/Gen24:62); [25:11](https://ref.ly/Gen25:11)). The name was a reminder of God’s care and help.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Genesis 29:33 (id: 12587)

Leah named her second son Simeon. The name suggests that she had cried out to the Lord and was heard (compare [16:11](https://ref.ly/Gen16:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 29:34 (id: 12588)

Leah named her third son Levi because she hoped her husband would become attached to her after she had borne him three sons. The name *Levi* sounds like the Hebrew word for “attached” or “joined.” But this did not happen.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`)

## Genesis 29:35 (id: 12589)

Leah accepted that Jacob would never love her. She named her fourth son Judah, saying, "I will praise the Lord." In Hebrew, *Judah* means "praised." She appeared to have stopped hoping for Jacob's love and found comfort in the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 29:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 30:1–2 (id: 12611)

In that culture, for a woman not to have children could feel like a kind of death (compare [1 Samuel 1](https://ref.ly/1Sam1:1-1Sam1:28)). Only God could enable Rachel to have children.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Be Zealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 30:1–8 (id: 12612)

Rachel’s naming of sons through Bilhah does not reflect faith as Leah’s namings had. Rachel felt wronged over the marriage and her barrenness. The names of Bilhah’s sons reflect Rachel’s bitter struggle with her sister and her feeling of some victory.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:1-8

Rachel decided to have children through her servant, and Jacob agreed. This is similar to Sarai using Hagar ([16:1–4](https://ref.ly/Gen16:1-Gen16:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bilhah (ID: `person:Bilhah`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

*Dan* means “he judged” or “he vindicated.” Rachel felt justified (Hebrew *dananni*) by Dan’s birth.

The name *Naphtali* is related to the phrase *In my great struggles.* In Hebrew, the phrase is *naptule ’elohim niptalti*, which means “I have struggled the struggles of God.”

## Genesis 30:9 (id: 12631)

When Leah saw that she had stopped having children, she gave her servant Zilpah to Jacob as a wife, even though she already had four sons.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:9

## Genesis 30:10–13 (id: 12613)

*Gad* (meaning "fortune") was the name of a god associated with fortune. *Asher* (meaning "happy") was the name of a god associated with luck. These names show Leah's pagan background, but there is no indication she believed in these gods.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:10-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Zilpah (ID: `person:Zilpah`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Gad (ID: `person:Gad`); Asher (ID: `person:Asher`)

## Genesis 30:14–17 (id: 12614)

People believed *mandrakes* could increase sexual desire and help women become pregnant (see [Song 7:13](https://ref.ly/Song7:13)). Rachel hoped they would help her become pregnant, so she made a deal with Leah. Rachel would let Leah spend one night with Jacob in exchange for mandrakes. Yet, Leah became pregnant, not Rachel.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:14-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Mandrake (ID: `flora:Mandrake`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Reuben (ID: `person:Reuben`); Wheat (ID: `flora:Wheat`)

## Genesis 30:18 (id: 12615)

The name *Issachar* reflects this idea of being hired or receiving payment. It reminds readers of the events surrounding his birth. Leah says that Jacob was "hired” for the night ([Genesis 30:16](https://ref.ly/Gen30:16)). The Hebrew word *sekari* means “my hire.”

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Issachar (ID: `person:Issachar`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`)

## Genesis 30:19–20 (id: 12616)

*Zebulun* means "honor" or "gift," like a dowry or tribute. Leah believed God gave her Zebulun so her husband would honor her. She always hoped this would happen.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:19-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Zebulun (ID: `person:Zebulun`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

Dinah was the only daughter of Jacob. See chapter [34](https://ref.ly/Gen34:1-Gen34:31).

## Genesis 30:22–24 (id: 12618)

* Rachel finally had her own son, Joseph, thanks to God's intervention. His birth was not due to superstitions ([30:14–16](https://ref.ly/Gen30:14-Gen30:16)) or the custom of giving servants as wives.
* The Hebrew word translated as "taken away" (*’asap*) sounds similar to the name *Joseph* (*yosep*), which means "may he add." Rachel was happy about Joseph's birth and prayed for another son.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:22-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`)

## Genesis 30:25–34 (id: 12619)

After working for fourteen years, Jacob asked Laban if he could return home. The two nomadic leaders negotiated politely but stayed cautious. Laban wanted more from Jacob, while Jacob wanted to earn his wages through selective breeding.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:25-34

## Genesis 30:27 (id: 12620)

*blessed me:* God made Laban successful because Jacob was with him (see [22:18](https://ref.ly/Gen22:18)). Laban may have looked for signs (omens) to understand what was happening, or he may simply have noticed the results. Dark\-colored sheep were uncommon ([30:32](https://ref.ly/Gen30:32)). So, people considered having many of this color as a sign of God's blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`)

## Genesis 30:30–33 (id: 12622)

Jacob agreed that God had blessed Laban through him, so he made a plan to gain something for himself. He proposed for his wages the rare black and multicolored goats and the speckled and spotted sheep that were born.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:30-33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 30:32 (id: 12623)

As Abraham had done with Lot ([13:9](https://ref.ly/Gen13:9)), Jacob gave Laban what he valued most. White sheep were more common and more valuable than dark or multicolored sheep; as a man of faith, Jacob was willing to take the rejects (compare [1 Corinthians 1:26](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:26)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 30:34–36 (id: 12624)

* Laban said he agreed with Jacob’s plan, but he tried to prevent Jacob from gaining wealth by removing animals from the flock that would fulfill the agreement.
* Laban's deception with the goats is similar to Jacob's deception with Esau (compare [27:9](https://ref.ly/Gen27:9); see study note on 29:14–30).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:34-36
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 29:14-30 (ID: `12576@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 30:37 (id: 12626)

*exposing the white inner wood:* This clever wordplay explains the entire section. When Jacob *exposed the white* (Hebrew *laban*) streaks of wood underneath, he played the "white" game (the *Laban* game) and won. By outsmarting Laban ("Whitey"), Jacob's flocks increased and he became successful.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Plane Tree (ID: `flora:PlaneTree`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Almond (ID: `flora:Almond`); Poplar (ID: `flora:Poplar`)

## Genesis 30:37–43 (id: 12625)

God blessed Jacob even though Laban was deceitful. Jacob cleverly used selective breeding to grow his flock. He followed the traditional belief that peeled sticks could affect the type of animals born. 

The peeled branches seemed to help his animals produce streaked and spotted offspring. Jacob later admitted that God had helped him succeed ([31:7–12](https://ref.ly/Gen31:7-Gen31:12)). Jacob ended up with stronger animals, while Laban got the weaker ones ([30:41–42](https://ref.ly/Gen30:41-Gen30:42)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:37-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Plane Tree (ID: `flora:PlaneTree`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Almond (ID: `flora:Almond`); Poplar (ID: `flora:Poplar`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Genesis 30:42 (id: 12627)

Laban now received what he deserved for how he treated Jacob. His attempt to cheat Jacob led to Jacob gaining more, because God was working in Jacob’s life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 30:42

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

Laban’s sons became more hostile toward Jacob because his flocks were growing faster than theirs. They were jealous because God was blessing Jacob. They were also afraid that he might take over everything they had.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`)

Jacob’s return journey led to a conflict with Laban. This conflict created a lasting boundary between Israel (Jacob) and Aram (Laban). God kept his promise to Jacob. He made Jacob successful in Paddan\-aram and protected him as he traveled back home.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:1-21

## Genesis 31:3 (id: 12644)

* *the land of your fathers* was Canaan, to which God had called Abraham ([12:1–7](https://ref.ly/Gen12:1-Gen12:7); [17:8](https://ref.ly/Gen17:8)).
* *Go back … I will be with you:* See study notes on 26:2–5; 28:12–15. God protected Jacob (also called “Israel,” [32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)) as he led his family back to the land God promised them. Later, God brought Israel back to Canaan after many years of slavery in Egypt.

    This return had many similarities to this passage:

    + God defeated foreign gods and beliefs.
        + He used dreams for rescue and protection.
        + He gave victory over threats.
        + He set boundaries between nations and tribes (see [Deuteronomy 32:8](https://ref.ly/Deut32:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:3
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 26:2-5 (ID: `12534@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Genesis 28:12-15 (ID: `12563@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 31:4–13 (id: 12645)

Jacob explained to his wives how God had blessed him, even though Laban opposed him. He was not sure if they wanted to leave Laban and move to Canaan. He wanted his family to agree willingly, so he needed to persuade them. He shared how God had guided and provided for them over the years. He explained that he needed to keep the vow he made at Bethel ([28:20–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:20-Gen28:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:4-13

The women quickly agreed to go with Jacob because God had blessed him. They were very willing to leave Laban, who had wasted their wealth (the property meant to support them). They understood that what God gave Jacob would also belong to them.

## Genesis 31:19–20 (id: 12636)

* *Rachel stole her father’s household idols:* She might have wanted to recover some of the wealth Laban wasted. She may have also worshiped idols (compare [35:2–4](https://ref.ly/Gen35:2-Gen35:4)). Having the idols might have meant claiming the family inheritance, as later customs indicate. Laban seemed to feel insecure without them. Whatever her reasons, Rachel's theft nearly caused disaster for the family when Laban overtook them.
* A wordplay shows that Rachel and Jacob were very similar—Rachel stole (Hebrew *wattignob*) Laban's household gods. Jacob tricked (Hebrew *wayyignob*, "stole the heart of, deceived") Laban.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:19-20

## Genesis 31:21 (id: 12637)

The journey took the family from Haran southwest to the land of Gilead, just east of the Jordan River in the north of today’s kingdom of Jordan.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Euphrates River (ID: `place:Euphrates`)

## Genesis 31:22–23 (id: 12638)

* The theft of the idols ([31:19](https://ref.ly/Gen31:19)) probably caused Laban and his men to chase Jacob. It was one thing for Jacob to take his family and flocks (Laban probably still thought they were his). But taking the household gods was different. Laban might have feared Jacob would return to claim all of Laban’s property. When he could not find the gods, he asked for a treaty to keep Jacob away ([31:43–53](https://ref.ly/Gen31:43-Gen31:53)).
* It took Laban seven days to overtake Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 31:24 (id: 12639)

*Be careful not to say anything to Jacob, either good or bad*: God told Laban not to try to bring justice himself. When we try to enact our own sense of good and evil apart from God’s command, we always do evil (see study note on 2:9).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:24
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 2:9 (ID: `12418@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Genesis 31:25–30 (id: 12640)

The dispute between the two men used the language of legal controversies and lawsuits (see also [31:36](https://ref.ly/Gen31:36)). Laban first argued that Jacob had wronged him by stealing from him.

Laban searched for the idols but did not find them. He did not think that a woman in her monthly bleeding would make the idols ceremonially unclean by sitting on them (see [Leviticus 15:19–24](https://ref.ly/Lev15:19-Lev15:24)).

## Genesis 31:40 (id: 12646)

Jacob, who preferred life at home ([25:27](https://ref.ly/Gen25:27))**,** had spent 20 years enduring the hardships of outdoor life, which Esau had loved.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:40


## Genesis 31:42 (id: 12647)

*the Fear of Isaac*: This is a name for God used here and in [31:53](https://ref.ly/Gen31:53). It does not mean that Isaac was afraid of God in a negative way. Here, *fear* means deep respect, honor, and awe for God.

God was with Jacob ([31:3](https://ref.ly/Gen31:3)). God saw Jacob’s hard work and faithfulness, even though Laban treated him badly. God rewarded Jacob. Laban’s dream showed Jacob that he was right.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:42; Genesis 31:53

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`)

## Genesis 31:43–44 (id: 12648)

Laban wanted a treaty to end the dispute because he felt unsafe and wanted to secure the borders. Jacob did not need a treaty because God had provided for him and protected him.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:43-44

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Laban (ID: `person:Laban`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 31:45–48 (id: 12649)

The stone and the pillar marked the border agreement between the two men. It was a witness for future generations. Each man named the monument *witness* in his own language. It became the lasting border between Israel and the kingdom of Aram (Syria), two nations often at war.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:45-48

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jegar-sahadutha (ID: `place:Jegar-sahadutha`); Laban (ID: `person:Laban`)

## Genesis 31:49 (id: 12650)

The pile of stones was also called Mizpah (which means “watchtower”). God would watch over Jacob and Laban to keep them apart, because they did not trust each other.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:49

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jegar-sahadutha (ID: `place:Jegar-sahadutha`)

## Genesis 31:50–53 (id: 12651)

Laban added conditions to the treaty to try to justify himself. He used many words to hide his own dishonesty and make Jacob look unethical. He even claimed credit for the monument Jacob had built ("here is the pillar I have set up," [31:51](https://ref.ly/Gen31:51)). The women and children would be safer and better cared for with Jacob than they ever were with Laban.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 31:50-53

God promised Jacob protection when he needed comfort the most. Jacob's journey was a physical return to his homeland and a spiritual return to God's promised land. God protects his people and fulfills his plan.

Apparently inspired by the vision of angels ([32:1](https://ref.ly/Gen32:1), Hebrew *mal’akim*), Jacob sent messengers (*mal’akim*) into Edom to meet Esau.

Jacob divided his company into two groups or camps (Hebrew *makhanoth*, related to "Mahanaim" in [32:2](https://ref.ly/Gen32:2)). This was because he was afraid. Jacob remembered Esau's character and his threat to kill him (see [25:25](https://ref.ly/Gen25:25); [27:41](https://ref.ly/Gen27:41)).

## Genesis 32:9–12 (id: 12668)

Jacob’s prayer is a wonderful example of how to address God. He based his appeal on God’s will, reminding God of his relationship with him, his command for him to return to the land, and his promise ([32:9](https://ref.ly/Gen32:9)). He had a correct attitude of genuine humility and total dependence on God ([32:10](https://ref.ly/Gen32:10)). Finally, he asked that God rescue him from his brother, and he repeated God’s promises (see [22:17](https://ref.ly/Gen22:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:9-12

## Genesis 32:22–24 (id: 12654)

The Jabbok River flows westward to the Jordan Valley, dividing the region of Bashan on the north from Gilead on the south. 

Wordplays on Jacob’s name and character preserve the memory of this encounter. Jacob (Hebrew ya‘aqob), while at Jabbok (Hebrew yabboq), wrestled (Hebrew wayye’abeq). Through his fight with an adversary to receive the blessing, Jacob’s name would be changed, and his deceptive striving would partially give way to faith as his way of life.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:22-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jabbok (ID: `place:Jabbok`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:22–32 (id: 12655)

Jacob went back to the land God promised him. Before this, God met, injured, and blessed him. God also changed his name to Israel. This event was a significant turning point for Jacob.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:22-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Penuel (ID: `place:Penuel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Jabbok (ID: `place:Jabbok`)

## Genesis 32:24 (id: 12656)

* *a man wrestled:* The story develops just as it did for Jacob. We do not know any details about the attacker, who later refused to reveal his identity ([32:29](https://ref.ly/Gen32:29)).
* *until daybreak:* The darkness matched Jacob’s situation, adding to his fear and uncertainty. In the dark, he could not tell who it was. It might have been one of Esau’s or Laban’s men.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:25 (id: 12657)

*he struck the socket of Jacob’s hip and dislocated it:* Jacob, who had struggled and deceived others, could no longer fight. When the man wrestled with him as an equal, Jacob could hold his ground. But now he met someone far stronger than himself.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:26 (id: 12658)

At daybreak, Jacob began to understand the meaning of the struggle. He realized who his opponent was. Since he could not win by fighting, he held on and asked for God’s blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`)

## Genesis 32:27 (id: 12659)

*What is your name?:* The Lord's question was about Jacob's character, not just his identity (see [3:9](https://ref.ly/Gen3:9); [4:9](https://ref.ly/Gen4:9)). By stating his name, Jacob admitted his nature and behavior as a "Heel\-grabber, Deceiver, Usurper." Before God would bless him, Jacob needed to recognize who he was. Then God would change his identity.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:28 (id: 12660)

* *Jacob:* See study note on 25:26.
* *Israel (“God fights”):* God first fought *with* him, but now God would fight *for* him. Jacob’s name promised hope for him and his descendants.
* *you have struggled with God and with men*: Throughout his life, Jacob tried to get God’s blessing using his own skills and by any means necessary. He understood the importance of the blessing but was too self\-reliant and proud to accept it freely. He had been fighting God long before this encounter.
* *and have prevailed:* He had succeeded in his struggles with Esau and with Laban. Now he received God’s blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:28
* **Associated Articles:** Genesis 25:26 (ID: `12517@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:29 (id: 12661)

Jacob knew who was with him ([32:30](https://ref.ly/Gen32:30)). The request was his attempt to regain some control. God would not reveal his name, which cannot be had on demand.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:30 (id: 12664)

* *Peniel* (which means “face of God”): The name shows that Jacob recognized the man as a manifestation of God (a *theophany*).
* *yet my life was spared* (or *and my soul has been rescued*): This probably meant Jacob realized that God had answered his prayer to be saved from Esau ([32:11](https://ref.ly/Gen32:11)). If he could encounter God like this and survive, he had nothing to fear from Esau. The saying may also reflect an ancient belief that no one can see God and live (see [Exodus 33:20](https://ref.ly/Exod33:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Penuel (ID: `place:Penuel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 32:31 (id: 12665)

God injured Jacob’s hip. This limited Jacob’s pride and self\-reliance (depending on his own strength). Because God reduced his natural strength, Jacob had to trust the Lord more.

Jacob thought he could return to his land by outsmarting his brother again ([32:3–21](https://ref.ly/Gen32:3-Gen32:21)). But at the border of the land, he met its true owner. He would receive the land, but only if God fought for him.

Self\-reliance (trying to gain God’s blessing by our own strength or by worldly ways) will not succeed. If we continue in this way, God may need to weaken our self\-reliance so that we trust him more.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Penuel (ID: `place:Penuel`)

## Genesis 32:32 (id: 12666)

The story includes a dietary restriction for Israel that became a custom but was not put into law. This custom helped preserve the memory of the story. Observant Jews still refuse to eat the tendons of an animal’s hindquarters.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 32:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 33:1–2 (id: 12670)

God had changed Jacob's identity ([32:27–28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:27-Gen32:28)), but he had not yet learned to live in line with his new name. He still showed favoritism (treating some family members better than others), which can divide families. 

He arranged his family and possessions according to their importance to him. He placed the servant wives and their children in front, where they would face danger first. Leah and her children were behind them. Rachel and Joseph were at the back, where it was safest.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 33:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 33:1–17 (id: 12669)

Jacob had long expected to meet his brother Esau, and the meeting turned out much better than he feared. Esau’s changed heart shows how “God fights” for his people (See [32:28](https://ref.ly/Gen32:28)). God works powerfully for his people when they trust him.

Earlier, Esau did not value his birthright (the rights and blessing of the firstborn son; [25:32–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:32-Gen25:34)). Now he no longer held on to old anger. Jacob understood that God had stepped in and changed the situation.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 33:1-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Esau (ID: `person:Esau`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Rachel (ID: `person:Rachel`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); To Be Kindly Disposed (ID: `keyterm:KindlyDisposed`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Seir (ID: `place:Seir`); Succoth (ID: `place:Succoth`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Genesis 33:3–13 (id: 12677)

Jacob was scared even though he had no reason to be. He tried to calm his brother by acting like a servant to a king:

* he bowed ([33:3](https://ref.ly/Gen33:3)),
* used respectful titles ([33:8](https://ref.ly/Gen33:8), [13](https://ref.ly/Gen33:13)),
* introduced people ([33:6–7](https://ref.ly/Gen33:6-Gen33:7)), and
* gave gifts ([33:8](https://ref.ly/Gen33:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 33:3-13

## Genesis 33:5 (id: 12679)

*your servant:* When speaking with his brother, Jacob kept calling himself Esau’s servant (also [33:14](https://ref.ly/Gen33:14)). He referred to Esau as his lord ([33:8](https://ref.ly/Gen33:8), [13–15](https://ref.ly/Gen33:13-Gen33:15)). Esau called Jacob “my brother” ([33:9](https://ref.ly/Gen33:9)). Jacob acted carefully to avoid any possible attack from Esau. In doing this, he went against the earlier oracle (a message from God about the future; [25:23](https://ref.ly/Gen25:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 33:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Be Kindly Disposed (ID: `keyterm:KindlyDisposed`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Genesis 34:1–2 (id: 12681)

* *Dinah* was probably Jacob's only daughter ([30:21](https://ref.ly/Gen30:21)).
* *Shechem … saw her, he took her and lay with her by force:* *Shechem* took Dinah and raped her, which ruined her chances for a proper marriage.

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Hivites (ID: `group:Hivites`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 34:1–31 (id: 12682)

After Jacob and his family settled in the land, the Canaanites became a threat. This story warns the Israelites about the risk of being influenced by the Canaanites. Later, God commanded Israel not to marry or make deals with them because they were corrupt and would corrupt others. This chapter gives an implicit warning against adopting their lifestyle ([34:1–2](https://ref.ly/Gen34:1-Gen34:2)). 

It also teaches Israel to maintain integrity and not misuse holy things for deceit or violence ([34:13](https://ref.ly/Gen34:13)). Israel's reputation was at risk in the land ([34:30](https://ref.ly/Gen34:30)). Because of their violent actions, Simeon and Levi did not receive the birthright blessing ([49:5–7](https://ref.ly/Gen49:5-Gen49:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:1-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Foolish (ID: `keyterm:Foolish`); Foreskin (ID: `keyterm:Foreskin`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Perizzites (ID: `group:Perizzites`); Leah (ID: `person:Leah`); Hivites (ID: `group:Hivites`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 34:3–4 (id: 12686)

Shechem had strong feelings for Dinah, and he was willing to go through great trouble because of her ([34:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen34:11-Gen34:12), [17–18](https://ref.ly/Gen34:17-Gen34:18), [24](https://ref.ly/Gen34:24)). However, the way he showed his “love” (not true love, but a selfish desire) was impulsive and self\-centered. It also broke accepted moral standards of that time (compare [Judges 14:2](https://ref.ly/Judg14:2); see [1 Corinthians 13:4–7](https://ref.ly/1Cor13:4-1Cor13:7); [1 John 4:10](https://ref.ly/1John4:10)). 

His character shows why Israel needed to stay separate from the Canaanites (the people living in the land at that time).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`)

## Genesis 34:5–7 (id: 12688)

Jacob’s response to this crime is surprising. When he heard that Dinah had been defiled, he said nothing about it until his sons came home. Because Jacob did not act, his sons did, though without the wisdom and integrity necessary for justice. 

Dinah’s full brothers were shocked and furious that Shechem had done such a disgraceful thing to Jacob’s family (literally *in Israel;* this is the first use of the name *Israel* to describe the family). This sexual crime was an outrage against the community of God’s people and deserved punishment, but the leader of the clan did nothing.

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:5-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Foolish (ID: `keyterm:Foolish`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`)

## Genesis 34:8–10 (id: 12689)

Hamor suggested a marriage alliance for mutual economic gain. But God had already promised Jacob everything, including the land ([34:10](https://ref.ly/Gen34:10); see [14:21–24](https://ref.ly/Gen14:21-Gen14:24)). Hamor wanted to control Israel’s wealth ([34:23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:23)). Trusting the Canaanites would not lead to anything good.

For some of these reasons, the law of Moses did not allow Israelites to marry Canaanites (see [Exodus 23:27–33](https://ref.ly/Exod23:27-Exod23:33); [Deuteronomy 7:1–5](https://ref.ly/Deut7:1-Deut7:5)). The exception would be if they converted (see [Joshua 2:1–15](https://ref.ly/Josh2:1-Josh2:15); [6:23–25](https://ref.ly/Josh6:23-Josh6:25); [Matthew 1:5](https://ref.ly/Matt1:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:8-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`)

## Genesis 34:13–17 (id: 12683)

Dinah’s brothers responded to the proposal without waiting for Jacob to respond, and they acted deceitfully. They may not have thought that Shechem and his people would ever agree to the rite of circumcision, but they knew what they would do if the Canaanites accepted ([34:25](https://ref.ly/Gen34:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:13-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Foreskin (ID: `keyterm:Foreskin`); Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`)

## Genesis 34:18–24 (id: 12684)

The Canaanite men agreed to the condition, and every male in the town was circumcised. This was not only to allow Shechem to marry Dinah. They also saw it as a way to gain everything that Jacob owned ([34:23](https://ref.ly/Gen34:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:18-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Circumcision (ID: `keyterm:Circumcision`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Genesis 34:25–29 (id: 12685)

When Simeon and Levi used circumcision (the sign of God’s covenant) to deceive and kill the Canaanite men, they showed disrespect for that sacred sign. Their killing of all the males and their looting of the city was not justice. It was harsh and excessive revenge.

They were angry and wanted to correct a wrong. But they should have asked for payment for the harm done (see [Exodus 22:16–17](https://ref.ly/Exod22:16-Exod22:17); [Deuteronomy 22:28–29](https://ref.ly/Deut22:28-Deut22:29)). Instead, their violent anger led to the loss of their birthright blessing (the special family blessing given to the firstborn; [Genesis 49:5–7](https://ref.ly/Gen49:5-Gen49:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dinah (ID: `person:Dinah`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Shechem (ID: `person:Shechem`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Simeon (ID: `person:Simeon.5`); Hamor (ID: `person:Hamor`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Genesis 34:30 (id: 12687)

Jacob responded again fearfully because of what might happen to him. But God made the people of the land fear him instead ([35:5](https://ref.ly/Gen35:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 34:30

## Genesis 35:1–7 (id: 12691)

Jacob returned to Bethel, about 24 kilometers (15 miles) south of Shechem, to fulfill the vows he made there ([28:20–22](https://ref.ly/Gen28:20-Gen28:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Genesis 35:1-7
